Global well-posedness and asymptotic behavior of large strong solutions to the 3D full compressible Navier-Stokes equations with temperature-dependent coefficients
Yachun Li, Peng Lu, Zhaoyang Shang

Abstract
It is well known that the global well-posedness of the Navier–Stokes equations with temperature-dependent coefficients is a challenging problem, especially in multi-dimensional space. In this paper, we study the 3D Navier–Stokes equations with temperature-dependent coefficients in the whole space, and establish the first result on the global existence of large strong solution when the initial density and the initial temperature are linearly equivalent to some large constant states. Moreover, the optimal decay rates of the solution to its associated equilibrium are established when the initial data belong to L p 0 ​ ( ℝ 3 ) L^{p_{0}}({\mathbb{R}}^{3}) for some p 0 ∈ [ 1 , 2 ] p_{0}\in[1,2] .



1
Introduction

The motion of a compressible viscous, heat-conductive, and Newtonian polytropic fluid is governed by the following full
compressible Navier-Stokes equations:

\begin{cases}\partial_{t}\rho+{\rm div}(\rho u)=0,\\
\partial_{t}(\rho u)+{\rm div}(\rho u\otimes u)+\nabla P={\rm div}\mathbb{T},\\
\partial_{t}(\rho\mathcal{E})+{\rm div}(\rho\mathcal{E}u+Pu)={\rm div}(u\mathbb{T})+{\rm div}(\tilde{\kappa}\nabla\theta),\end{cases}

(1.1)

where
x=(x_{1},x_{2},x_{3})^{\top}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{3}
and
t\geq 0
denote the spatial coordinate and time coordinate,
\rho\geq 0
the mass density,
u=(u^{1},u^{2},u^{3})^{\top}
the fluid velocity,
P
the pressure of the fluid,
\theta
the absolute temperature,
\mathcal{E}=e+\frac{1}{2}|u|^{2}
the specific total energy,
e
the specific internal energy. The equation of state for polytropic fluids satisfies

P=R\rho\theta,\quad e=c_{v}\theta,

(1.2)

where
R
is the gas constant, and
c_{v}
is the specific heat at
constant volume.
\mathbb{T}
is the viscosity stress tensor given by
\mathbb{T}=2\tilde{\mu}\mathfrak{D}(u)+\tilde{\lambda}{\rm div}u\mathbb{I}_{3}
, where

\mathfrak{D}(u)=\frac{\nabla u+(\nabla u)^{\top}}{2}

is the deformation tensor,
\mathbb{I}_{3}
is the
3\times 3
identity matrix,
\tilde{\mu}
is the shear viscosity coefficient, and
\tilde{\lambda}+\frac{2}{3}\tilde{\mu}
is the bulk viscosity coefficient.
\tilde{\kappa}
denotes the coefficient of thermal conductivity.
In this paper, we will consider the following temperature-dependent transport coefficients:

\tilde{\mu}(\theta)=\mu\theta^{\alpha},\quad\tilde{\lambda}(\theta)=\lambda\theta^{\alpha},\quad\tilde{\kappa}=\kappa\theta^{\beta},

(1.3)

where
\mu
,
\lambda
,
\alpha
and
\beta
are constants satisfying
\mu>0,2\mu+3\lambda\geq 0,\alpha>0,\beta>0
.

When the viscosity coefficients are constants, there have been a lot of literature on the well-posedness theory for the full compressible Navier-Stokes equations (
1.1
) in multi-dimensional space. The local existence and uniqueness of strong and classical solutions in bounded and unbounded domains were established in
[
24
,
45
,
48
]
and for the global existence of weak solutions, we refer to
[
12
,
17
,
18
,
19
,
20
]
. For the global well-posedness results of strong and smooth solutions in three-dimensional space, in 1979, Matsumura and Nishida
[
42
]
first demonstrated the global strong solutions with the initial data close to a non-vacuum equilibrium in Sobolev space, see also
[
43
,
44
]
. In 1996, Jiang
[
25
]
considered the equations in the domain exterior to a ball and proved the global existence of spherically symmetric smooth solutions for large initial data with spherical symmetry. In 1998, Xin
[
58
]
presented a sufficient condition on the blowup of smooth solution in arbitrary space dimensions with initial density of compact support. It is shown that any smooth solution for polytropic fluids in the absence of heat conduction will blow up in finite time as long as the initial density has compact support. In 2013, Xin and Yan
[
60
]
improved the blowup results of
[
58
]
by removing the crucial assumptions that the initial density has compact support and the smooth solution has finite total energy. In 2017, Wen and Zhu
[
57
]
investigated the Cauchy problem and showed that the strong solution exists globally in time if the initial mass is small for the fixed coefficients of viscosity
\mu
and heat conductivity
\kappa
. In the next year, Huang and Li
[
23
]
proved the global existence of classical solution to the Cauchy problem with smooth initial data which are of small energy but possibly large oscillations, see also
[
32
]
given by Lai, Xu and Zhang for the case that the far-field limit is equal to zero.
Recently, Chen, Huang and Zhu
[
5
]
established the global-in-time existence of solution of the Cauchy problem with initial data that are large, discontinuous, spherically symmetric, and away from the vacuum. The solution they obtained is of global finite total energy and on any region strictly away from the possible vacuum, the velocity and specific internal energy are Hölder continuous, and the density has a uniform upper bound. For the cases of global well-posedness in 1D and 2D spaces, we refer to
[
26
,
27
,
29
,
33
,
34
,
35
,
36
,
49
]
, etc., and the references cited therein.

However, the viscosity coefficients are not necessarily constants, they may depend on density or temperature, or both. We first review some well-posedness results when viscosities depend only on the density. In 1985, Kawohl
[
28
]
proved the global existence of large classical solution to initial-boundary value problem in one dimensional space when the transport coefficients satisfy

0<\mu_{0}\leq\tilde{\mu}(v)\leq\mu_{1},\quad\kappa_{0}(1+\theta^{q})\leq\tilde{\kappa}(v,\theta)\leq\kappa_{1}(1+\theta^{q}),\quad|\tilde{\kappa}_{v}(v,\theta)|+|\tilde{\kappa}_{vv}(v,\theta)|\leq\kappa_{1}(1+\theta^{q}),

(1.4)

where
v
is specific volume and
q\geq 2
.
In 2016, Wang
[
55
]
studied the initial and initial-boundary value problems for the
p
-th power Newtonian fluid and established the existence and uniqueness of global smooth non-vacuum solutions when the transport coefficients
\mu
and
\kappa
are given as

\tilde{\mu}(\rho)=\rho^{\alpha},\quad\tilde{\kappa}(\theta)=\theta^{\beta},

(1.5)

with some positive parameters
\alpha
and
\beta
.
In 2017, Duan, Guo and Zhu
[
8
]
considered the initial-boundary value problem with the stress-free and heat insulated boundary condition, and established the global existence of strong solution with the initial density is away from vacuum and the following density-dependent viscosity and temperature-dependent heat conductivity

\tilde{\mu}(\rho)=1+\rho^{\alpha},\quad\tilde{\kappa}(\theta)=\theta^{\beta},\quad\alpha\geq 0,\quad\beta>0.

(1.6)

For the three dimensional case, in 2007, Bresch and Desjardîns
[
2
]
considered the Cauchy problem and periodic problem, and proved that the weak solutions exist globally in time under the condition

\tilde{\lambda}(\rho)=2(\tilde{\mu}^{\prime}(\rho)\rho-\tilde{\mu}(\rho)),\quad\tilde{\kappa}(\rho,\theta)=\kappa_{0}(\rho,\theta)(1+\rho)(1+\theta^{\alpha}),\quad\alpha\geq 2.

In 2024, Li, Lu, Shang and Yu
[
37
]
studied the Cauchy problem with the following
density-dependent viscosities

\tilde{\mu}(\rho)=\rho^{\alpha},\quad\tilde{\lambda}(\rho)=\rho^{\alpha},\quad\tilde{\kappa}=constant>0,\quad\alpha>4.

(1.7)

By using the modified effective viscous flux and using the bootstrap
argument, they established the global existence of large strong solution when the initial density is linearly equivalent to a large constant state. Very recently, Shen, Xu and Zhang
[
50
]
considered the Dirichlet initial-boundary value problem with density-dependent viscosities
\tilde{\mu}(\rho),\tilde{\lambda}(\rho)\in C^{1}
. The global well-posedness of strong solutions with vacuum is established provided that the initial total energy
\|\sqrt{\rho_{0}}u_{0}\|_{L^{2}}^{2}+\|\rho_{0}\theta_{0}\|_{L^{1}}
is suitably small.

In the above, we introduced some well-posedness results on the density-dependent viscosity coefficients. In fact, the system (
1.1
) can be deduced from the Boltzmann equation through the Chapman-Enskog expansion to the second order, under some proper physical assumptions, the transport coefficients are power functions of temperature of the form (
1.3
). On the other hand, experimental evidence
[
28
,
63
]
pointed out that the transport coefficients usually depend on temperature, in particular, they increase as (
1.3
) when the temperature is high. In this case, it is challenging to prove the global well-posedness of strong or smooth solutions to the nonisentropic compressible Navier–Stokes equations, most known results are for one-dimensional case. In 2014, Liu, Yang, Zhao and Zou
[
38
]
considered the Cauchy problem, and first obtained the existence and uniqueness of a globally smooth nonvacuum solution provided that

(\gamma-1)\|(v_{0}-1,u_{0},s_{0}-1)\|_{H^{3}}\leq C,

where
\gamma
is the adiabatic exponent. When
\gamma
is close to 1, the initial norm
\|(v_{0}-1,u_{0},s_{0}-1)\|_{H^{3}}
can be large. In 2016, Wang and Zhao
[
56
]
considered the case that the transport coefficients are given by

\tilde{\mu}=\mu_{0}h(v)\theta^{\alpha},\quad\tilde{\kappa}=\kappa_{0}h(v)\theta^{\alpha},

(1.8)

where
\mu_{0}>0
,
\kappa_{0}>0
and
\alpha
are constants,
h(v)
is a smooth function of the specific volume
v
satisfying

v^{l_{1}}+v^{-l_{2}}\leq Ch(v),\quad h^{\prime}(v)^{2}v\leq Ch(v)^{3},\quad\text{for all}\quad v\in(0,+\infty),

(1.9)

for some
C>0
and
l_{1}\geq 3
,
l_{2}\geq 3
, they proved the existence and uniqueness of the global-in-time classical solution to the initial-boundary value problem when the exponent
|\alpha|
is sufficiently small.
In 2021, Sun, Zhang and Zhao
[
51
]
considered the case that the transport coefficients are given by

\tilde{\mu}(\theta)=\mu_{0}\theta^{\alpha},\quad\tilde{\kappa}(\theta)=\kappa_{0}\theta^{\beta},

(1.10)

where
\mu_{0}
and
\kappa_{0}
are positive constants and
\alpha\geq 0
,
\beta\geq 0
. They proved the global-in-time existence of strong solutions to the initial-boundary value problem when
\alpha
is sufficiently small.
Recently, the Cauchy problem was investigated by Dong and Guo in
[
7
]
.

In three-dimensional space, there are rich literature of global weak solutions with large initial data. In 2012, Feireisl, Mucha, Novotný and Pokorný
[
10
]
proved the existence of at least one time-periodic weak solution to the initial-boundary value problem,
where the transport coefficients are supposed to be continuously differentiable functions of
\theta
satisfying

\underline{\mu}(1+\theta)\leq\tilde{\mu}(\theta),\quad|\tilde{\mu}^{\prime}(\theta)|\leq C,\quad 0\leq\tilde{\lambda}(\theta)\leq\overline{\lambda}(1+\theta),

(1.11)

\underline{\kappa}(1+\theta^{3})\leq\tilde{\kappa}(\theta)\leq\overline{\kappa}(1+\theta^{3}),

(1.12)

where
\underline{\mu}
,
\overline{\lambda}
,
\underline{\kappa}
and
\overline{\kappa}
are positive constants. Feireisl and Novotný
[
11
]
proved the weak–strong uniqueness of initial-boundary value problem with the transport coefficients satisfying

\tilde{\mu}(\theta)=\mu_{0}+\mu_{1}\theta,\quad\tilde{\lambda}(\theta)=0,\quad\tilde{\kappa}(\theta)=\kappa_{0}+\kappa_{2}\theta^{2}+\kappa_{3}\theta^{3},

for some positive constants
\mu_{0}
,
\mu_{1}
,
\kappa_{0}
,
\kappa_{2}
,
\kappa_{3}
.
In 2022, Chaudhuri and Feireisl
[
4
]
considered general non-homogeneous Dirichlet boundary conditions for
u
and
\theta
, and introduced a new concept of weak solution satisfying the entropy and energy inequalities. When the transport coefficients are supposed to be continuously differentiable functions of
\theta
satisfying

\underline{\mu}(1+\theta^{\alpha})\leq\tilde{\mu}(\theta)\leq\overline{\mu}(1+\theta^{\alpha}),\quad|\tilde{\mu}^{\prime}(\theta)|\leq C,\quad 0\leq\tilde{\lambda}(\theta)\leq\overline{\lambda}(1+\theta^{\alpha}),

(1.13)

\underline{\kappa}(1+\theta^{\beta})\leq\tilde{\kappa}(\theta)\leq\overline{\kappa}(1+\theta^{\beta}).

(1.14)

for
\frac{1}{2}\leq\alpha\leq 1
and
\beta=3
, they established the weak–strong uniqueness and the existence of global-in-time weak solutions. In 2024, Feireisl, Lu and Sun
[
9
]
studied the initial-boundary value problem with
\alpha=1
and
\beta>6
in (
1.13
)–(
1.14
), and proved the global stability of an equilibrium with small perturbations. For the well-posedness results of strong solutions, in 2020, Yu and Zhang
[
61
]
studied the initial-boundary value problem in a smooth bounded domain
\Omega
with Dirichlet boundary condition

(u,\theta)(x,t)=(0,0),\quad\text{on}\quad\partial\Omega\times(0,T),

and density–temperature–dependent viscosities

\tilde{\mu}(\rho,\theta),\tilde{\lambda}(\rho,\theta)\in C^{1}({\mathbb{R}}^{2}),\quad\tilde{\mu}(\rho,\theta)>0,\quad 2\tilde{\mu}(\rho,\theta)+3\tilde{\lambda}(\rho,\theta)\geq 0,

and proved the global existence of strong solution provided that
\|\nabla u_{0}\|_{L^{2}}^{2}+\|\nabla\theta_{0}\|_{L^{2}}^{2}
is sufficiently small. In 2022, Cao and Li
[
3
]
established the local well-posedness of strong solution to the initial-boundary value problem with the transport coefficients are given in (
1.3
) with
\alpha,\beta\geq 0
, where the initial density is allowed to contain vacuum. In 2024, Hou, Liu, Wang and Xu
[
21
]
studied the vanishing viscosity limit for Navier–Stokes equations with temperature-dependent transport coefficients satisfying (
1.3
) for
\alpha=\beta>0
.

From the above mentioned known results, we can see that there are already some literature concerning the well-posedness of nonisentropic compressible Navier-Stokes equations with variable coefficients. However, compared to the case of constant coefficients and density-dependent viscosities, there are much fewer results with temperature-dependent transport coefficients, which are more in line with physical experiments, especially in multi-dimensional case. In this paper, we are interested in investigating the global well-posedness and asymptotic behavior of strong solution to the system (
1.1
) with temperature-dependent transport coefficients and large initial data in three-dimensional space. Without loss of generality, we assume the constant
c_{v}=1
in (
1.2
). It follows from (
1.2
)–(
1.3
) that (
1.1
) can be rewritten as

\begin{cases}\partial_{t}\rho+{\rm div}(\rho u)=0,\\
\rho(\partial_{t}u+(u\cdot\nabla)u)+\nabla P=2\mu{\rm div}(\theta^{\alpha}\mathfrak{D}(u))+\lambda\nabla(\theta^{\alpha}{\rm div}u),\\
\rho(\partial_{t}\theta+u\cdot\nabla\theta)+P{\rm div}u=2\mu\theta^{\alpha}|\mathfrak{D}(u)|^{2}+\lambda\theta^{\alpha}({\rm div}u)^{2}+\kappa{\rm div}(\theta^{\beta}\nabla\theta).\end{cases}

(1.15)

When system (
1.15
) is supplemented with the following initial data and far field behavior

\displaystyle(\rho,u,\theta)(x,0)=(\rho_{0},u_{0},\theta_{0})(x)\quad\text{for}\quad x\in{\mathbb{R}}^{3},

(1.16)

\displaystyle(\rho,u,\theta)(t,x)\to\left(\bar{\rho},0,\bar{\theta}\right)\quad\text{as}\quad|x|\to\infty\quad\text{for}\quad t\geq 0,

where
\bar{\rho}
and
\bar{\theta}
are positive constants, we have our main theorem as follows.

Theorem 1.1
.

(
Global existence of strong solution
)

Let the positive constants
\bar{\rho},\bar{\theta}>1
,
2\leq\alpha\leq 10
, and
\alpha<\beta<\alpha+2
. Suppose the initial data
(\rho_{0},u_{0},\theta_{0})
satisfy

\frac{3}{4}\bar{\rho}\leq\rho_{0}(x)\leq\frac{5}{4}\bar{\rho},\quad\frac{3}{4}\bar{\theta}\leq\theta_{0}(x)\leq\frac{5}{4}\bar{\theta},\quad\left(\rho_{0}(x)-\bar{\rho},~u_{0}(x),~\theta_{0}(x)-\bar{\theta}\right)\in H^{2},

(1.17)

\|\rho_{0}-\bar{\rho}\|_{H^{2}}+\|\theta_{0}-\bar{\theta}\|_{H^{2}}\leq C_{0},

(1.18)

for some positive constant
C_{0}
independent of
\bar{\rho}
and
\bar{\theta}
. Then there exist positive constants
\widetilde{L}
independent of
\bar{\rho}
,
\bar{\theta}
, and
L(\bar{\rho})
independent of
\bar{\theta}
, such that if
\bar{\rho}\geq\widetilde{L}
and
\bar{\theta}\geq L(\bar{\rho})
, the Cauchy problem (
1.15
)–(
1.16
) has a global strong solution
(\rho,u,\theta)
satisfying

\displaystyle\big(\rho-\bar{\rho},u,\theta-\bar{\theta}\big)\in C([0,\infty);H^{2}),\quad u\in L^{2}(0,\infty;W^{2,4}),

(1.19)

\displaystyle\rho_{t}\in L^{\infty}(0,\infty;H^{1}),\quad({u}_{t},\theta_{t})\in L^{\infty}(0,\infty;L^{2})\cap L^{2}(0,\infty;H^{1}),

and

\frac{2}{3}\bar{\rho}\leq\rho(x,t)\leq\frac{4}{3}\bar{\rho},\quad\frac{2}{3}\bar{\theta}\leq\theta(x,t)\leq\frac{4}{3}\bar{\theta},\quad\text{for}\quad(x,t)\in{\mathbb{R}}^{3}\times(0,\infty).

(1.20)

Remark 1.1
.

We would like to mention that Theorem
1.1
is the first result concerning the global existence of strong solution for the Cauchy problem to the 3D full Navier-Stokes equation with temperature-dependent coefficients and large initial data. Our global strong solution in solution space (
1.19
) is uniformly bounded which independent of time
T
. The uniform-in-time lower and upper bounds of the density and temperature are also given in (
1.20
). In fact, applying the argument in
[
23
]
, our global strong solution can be a classical one, we omit the detail proof for brevity.

Remark 1.2
.

In Theorem
1.1
, we consider the transport coefficients depending on the temperature (
1.3
). This is certainly a restriction which is physically motivated, experimental evidence
[
28
,
63
]
pointed out that the coefficients of viscosity and heat
conductivity usually depend on temperature. In particular, the transport coefficients increase such as (
1.3
) when the temperature is high, which is highly consistent with our assumption regarding the initial temperature is linearly equivalent to some large constant states.
The restrictions upon the parameters
2\leq\alpha\leq 10
,
\alpha<\beta<\alpha+2
are technical requirements. They are used to controlling the
(\bar{\rho},\bar{\theta})
-dependent nonlinear terms in the a priori estimates (see Proposition
3
). In fact, the cases where the fluid is an ionized gas,
\alpha=\frac{5}{2}
, and partial situational of intermolecular potential varies as
r^{-b}
with
r
being the molecule distance,
\alpha=\frac{1}{2}+\frac{2}{b},b\in(0,+\infty]
, are included in our results. It should be noted that the above restrictions upon the parameters
\alpha,\beta
are not sharp, it would be interesting to study the problem (
1.15
)–(
1.16
) when
0<\alpha<2
and
\beta\leq\alpha
. This is left for interested readers.

Now, we turn to the large time behavior of the large strong solution. We first review some related known results. When the transport coefficients are constants, we refer to
[
30
,
42
,
43
,
46
]
and
[
15
,
16
,
40
,
41
,
53
,
65
]
for decay rates in the case that the initial perturbation is sufficiently small or not, respectively. When the viscosity coefficients depend on the temperature, there are much fewer results about the large time behavior of global solutions. For the case of weak solutions, in 2007, Feireisl and Petzeltová
[
13
]
considered the equation (
1.1
) in a three dimensional bounded domain
\Omega
with the transport coefficients satisfying (
1.11
)–(
1.12
). When the system is supplied with an external force
f=\nabla F
for some
F(x)\in W^{1,\infty}(\Omega)
, they proved that the global weak solution
(\rho,\rho u)
converges to a non-constant equilibrium
(\bar{\rho},0)
in
L^{1}(\Omega)
as time goes to infinity, and

\lim\limits_{t\to+\infty}\int_{\Omega}\rho s(\rho,\theta){\mathrm{d}}x=\int\bar{\rho}s(\bar{\rho},\bar{\theta}){\mathrm{d}}x,

where
s(\rho,\theta)
is the entropy, and the equilibrium
(\bar{\rho},\bar{\theta})
solves the equation
\nabla P(\bar{\rho},\bar{\theta})=\bar{\rho}\nabla F
. In 2013, Bella, Feireisl and Pražák
[
1
]
considered the same problem when the driving force takes the form
f(t,x)=t^{a}\omega(t^{b})F(x)
with
\omega\in L^{\infty}({\mathbb{R}})
,
F\in W^{1,\infty}(\Omega)
and
b>a+2+\max\{a,0\}
. They proved that the global weak solution
(\rho,\rho u,\theta)
satisfies

\lim\limits_{t\to+\infty}\left(\|\rho-\bar{\rho}\|_{L^{\frac{5}{3}}}+\|\rho u\|_{L^{\frac{5}{4}}}+\|\theta-\bar{\theta}\|_{L^{4}}\right)=0,

for some constant equilibrium
(\bar{\rho},0,\bar{\theta})
. In 2024, Chiodaroli and Feireisl
[
6
]
considered the three dimensional Cauchy problem with far-field limit
(0,0,\bar{\theta})
and transport coefficients satisfying (
1.11
) and

\tilde{\kappa}(\theta)=\kappa_{0}\theta+\kappa_{1}\theta^{3}.

They proved that any global weak solutions to the system approaches the equilibrium in the sense that

\lim\limits_{T\to+\infty}\frac{1}{T}\int_{1}^{T}\left(\|\rho\|_{L^{\frac{5}{3}}}^{\frac{5}{3}}+\|u\|_{H^{1}}^{2}+\|\theta-\bar{\theta}\|_{H^{1}}^{2}\right){\mathrm{d}}t=0.

For the results of large time behavior of global strong or smooth solutions, they mainly focus on the one-dimensional and spherically or cylindrically symmetric situations. In 2014, Liu, Yang, Zhao and Zou
[
38
]
proved that the global classical solution
(v,u,\theta)
of one-dimensional Cauchy problem with
\tilde{\mu}(\theta)>0,~\tilde{\kappa}(\theta)>0
satisfies

\lim\limits_{t\to+\infty}\|(v-\bar{v},u,\theta-\bar{\theta})\|_{L^{\infty}}=0,

similar results are obtained by Wang and Zhao
[
56
]
in 2016 with the assumptions (
1.8
)–(
1.9
).
In 2020, Yu and Zhang
[
61
]
proved that the global strong solution
(\rho,u,\theta)
of three-dimensional initial boundary value problem with
\tilde{\mu}(\rho,\theta)>0,~\tilde{\kappa}(\rho,\theta)>0
satisfies

\displaystyle\|\sqrt{\rho}u\|_{L^{2}}^{2}+\|\sqrt{\rho}\theta\|_{L^{2}}^{2}\leq~

\displaystyle Ce^{-Ct},

\displaystyle\|\sqrt{\rho}u_{t}\|_{L^{2}}^{2}+\|\sqrt{\rho}\theta_{t}\|_{L^{2}}^{2}+\|\nabla u\|_{L^{2}}^{2}+\|\nabla\theta\|_{L^{2}}^{2}\leq~

\displaystyle C(1+t)^{-2}.

In
[
51
]
, Sun, Zhang and Zhao studied the global strong solution of one-dimensional initial boundary value problem with (
1.10
), and proved that the
H^{1}
-norm of the solution is exponential decay. Very recently, Dong and Guo
[
7
]
studied the one-dimensional Cauchy problem with (
1.10
), and proved that the global strong solution
(v,u,\theta)
satisfies

\lim\limits_{t\to+\infty}\left(\|(v-\bar{v},u,\theta-\bar{\theta})\|_{L^{q}}+\|(v_{x},u_{x},\theta_{x})\|_{H^{1}}\right)=0,

for any
2<q\leq\infty
. However, to the authors’ best knowledge, there are few results concerning the optimal decay rate for the Cauchy problem of (
1.15
)–(
1.16
) with variable coefficients.

Motivated by the above results, in the following, we give our second result concerning the algebraic decay rates of the global solution obtained in Theorem
1.1
.

Theorem 1.2
.

(
Decay rates of the solution
)

Let
(\rho,u,\theta)
be the global strong solution of (
1.15
)–(
1.16
) obtained in Theorem
1.1
. Suppose that
(\rho_{0}-\bar{\rho},u_{0},\theta_{0}-\bar{\theta})\in L^{p_{0}}({\mathbb{R}}^{3})
for some
p_{0}\in[1,2]
, then we have

\big\|\big(\rho-\bar{\rho},u,\theta-\bar{\theta}\big)\big\|_{L^{2}}\leq C(\bar{\rho},\bar{\theta})(1+t)^{-\frac{3}{4}(\frac{2}{p_{0}}-1)},

(1.21)

\|\left(\nabla\rho,\nabla u,\nabla\theta\right)\|_{L^{2}}+\|(\rho_{t},u_{t},\theta_{t})\|_{L^{2}}\leq C(\bar{\rho},\bar{\theta})(1+t)^{-\frac{3}{4}(\frac{2}{p_{0}}-1)-\frac{1}{2}},

(1.22)

\|\left(\nabla^{2}\rho,\nabla^{2}u,\nabla^{2}\theta\right)\|_{L^{2}}\leq C(\bar{\rho},\bar{\theta})(1+t)^{-\frac{3}{4}(\frac{2}{p_{0}}-1)-1},

(1.23)

where the constant
C(\bar{\rho},\bar{\theta})
depends on
\bar{\rho}
,
\bar{\theta}
,
\mu
,
\lambda
,
\kappa
,
R
and the initial data.

Remark 1.3
.

In the previous works
[
1
,
6
,
7
,
13
,
22
,
38
,
54
,
56
]
, the authors established the long-time behavior of solutions to full compressible Navier-Stokes equations with temperature-dependent coefficients. More precisely, they proved that the solution converges to an equilibrium in some
L^{p}
and
H^{2}
spaces as time goes to infinity. However, the explicit decay rates are not given. In this paper, we establish the decay rates of the first-order and the second-order derivatives of large strong solution when the initial data
(\rho_{0}-\bar{\rho},u_{0},\theta_{0}-\bar{\theta})
only belongs to
H^{2}
. Moreover, if
(\rho_{0}-\bar{\rho},u_{0},\theta_{0}-\bar{\theta})\in L^{p_{0}}
for some
p_{0}\in[1,2)
, additionally, we obtain the optimal decay rates of large strong solution in
H^{2}
space. As a result, we improve the results in
[
1
,
6
,
7
,
13
,
22
,
38
,
54
,
56
]
. As far as we know, this is the first result concerning the decay ratescc of full compressible Navier-Stokes equations with temperature-dependent coefficients in the whole space.

We now make some comments on the analysis of this paper. The local
existence and uniqueness of strong solutions to (
1.15
)–(
1.16
) can be found in
[
24
,
45
,
48
,
52
,
64
]
. In this paper, we continue the works in
[
37
]
and study the global well-posedenss of strong solutions to 3D nonisentropic Naiver-Stokes equations with temperature-dependent coefficients and large initial data. Firstly, we consider the global existence of large strong solutions to the Cauchy problem (
1.15
)–(
1.16
) when initial data satisfy (
1.17
)–(
1.18
) and both initial density and initial temperature are linearly equivalent to a large constant state. Due to the dependence of the transport coefficients on the temperature, we need large initial temperature, additionally, to control the possible increase of the temperature. The key difficulties in this paper are to derive the time-independent bounds of the temperature and the first-order spatial derivatives of the density. We show that, in the process of our a priori estimates, the parameter
\alpha
cannot exceed the given constant, which is different from the density-dependent viscosities case in
[
37
]
. Secondly, based on the global large strong solution established above, we consider the optimal decay rates of the solution to its associated equilibrium when the initial data belong to
L^{p_{0}}({\mathbb{R}}^{3})
,
p_{0}\in[1,2)
and far away from the equilibrium. More precisely,

•

Global existence of large strong solution.

In the lower-order estimates, we first give an entropy-type inequality, and the bounds on
L^{2}
-norm of the solution are obtained. Here, the bounds are uniform-in-time and depend on the far-field limits of density
\bar{\rho}
and temperature
\bar{\theta}
, which imply the smallness of
\nabla u
and
\nabla\theta
in
L^{2}(0,T;L^{2})
, and will play a crucial role in the analysis of this paper. In this paper, the first main difficulty is caused by the viscosity related nonlinear terms. To overcome this difficulty, different from the cases of constant coefficients
[
23
]
and density-dependent viscosities
[
37
]
, we introduce the following new effective viscous flux

G=(2\mu+\lambda){\rm div}u-R(\rho\theta^{1-\alpha}-\bar{\rho}\bar{\theta}^{1-\alpha}),

by which, after some careful analysis on the standard
L^{p}
elliptic estimate, we succeed in deriving some new uniform estimates on the key terms of
\|\nabla u\|_{L^{6}}
and
\|\nabla G\|_{L^{p}}
for
p\in[2,6]
. The second main difficulty comes from the estimates of
\|\sqrt{\rho}\dot{u}\|_{L^{2}}
and
\|\sqrt{\rho}\dot{\theta}\|_{L^{2}}
. When the transport coefficients depend on temperature and
\theta
is large, by the standard energy method, we find that
\|\sqrt{\rho}\dot{u}\|_{L^{2}}
and
\|\sqrt{\rho}\dot{\theta}\|_{L^{2}}
may be large terms, where
\|\sqrt{\rho}\dot{u}\|_{L^{2}}
plays an important role in the higher-order estimates of the strong solution. When the upper bound of
\|\sqrt{\rho}\dot{u}\|_{L^{2}}
is too large, we cannot use bootstrap argument to close our a priori assumptions directly. For this reason, we divide our estimates with respect to time into two parts
(0,\sigma(T))
and
(\sigma(T),T)
, and some new difficulties come out. In the time interval
(0,\sigma(T))
, estimates on the first-order and the second-order spatial derivative of
(u,\theta)
, (see
A_{i}(\sigma(T))(i=1,\cdots,4)
), with elaborate analysis on the power of
\bar{\theta}
, can be obtained provided that
\bar{\theta}
is suitably large. Next, in the time interval
(\sigma(T),T)
, the time-weighted estimates on the first-order and the second-order spatial derivative of
(u,\theta)
, (see
B_{i}(\sigma(T))(i=1,\cdots,4)
) are proved provided that
\bar{\theta}
is suitably large. It should be noted that the estimates on
B_{i}(\sigma(T))
are better than
A_{i}(\sigma(T))
due to the different initial values. With the estimates on the first-order and the second-order spatial derivatives of temperature at hand, we show that, by using Gagliardo–Nirenberg inequality, the lower and upper bounds of temperature can be controlled by
\bar{\theta}
. At the end of our a priori estimates, we are in position to estimate the first-order spatial derivatives of density, see
A_{5}(T)
and
A_{6}(T)
, which is the third main difficulty in our proof. Different from the density-dependent viscosities case in
[
37
]
, when coefficients depend on temperature, the largeness of
\bar{\theta}
is not enough to control the possible growth of
\|\nabla\rho\|_{L^{2}}
. After careful observation, we find that there exists dissipative property when we take the first-order energy estimate on the density provided that
\bar{\rho}
is suitably large in addition.
At last, together with
L^{2}
-norm estimate of
\rho-\bar{\rho}
and Gagliardo–Nirenberg inequality, the lower and upper bounds of the density can be obtained, which depend only on the
\bar{\rho}
. Based on the analysis mentioned above, we can close the a priori assumptions in Proposition
3
. In Lemma
3.2
, different from the previous results
[
23
,
42
,
43
]
, the
(\bar{\rho},\bar{\theta})
-dependent uniform upper bounds of the strong solution are given, and hence, global existence of large strong solution is established.

•

Asymptotic behavior of large strong solution.

In Theorem
1.1
, we establish the global-in-time existence of large strong solutions to the Cauchy problem (
1.15
)–(
1.16
). Motivated by results in
[
14
,
15
,
16
,
40
,
53
,
59
,
65
]
, in Theorem
1.2
, we turn to consider the decay rates of global strong solution to the full compressible Navier-Stokes equations with temperature-dependent coefficients and large data. To establish the
L^{2}
-optimal decay rates of the solution, the first step is to derive a dissipative inequality by suitable linear combinations of the energy estimates. Due to the temperature-dependent coefficients, uniform estimates on
\|\nabla\rho\|_{L^{2}}
and
\|\nabla\rho\|_{L^{4}}
are needed, additionally, which are not given in
[
15
,
16
]
. The second step is to obtain low-frequency estimates of the solution, where the estimates on the nonlinear terms

{\rm div}\mathbb{T}

\textstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt

and

{\rm div}(\theta^{\beta}\nabla\theta)

\textstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt

become more complicated, see (
4.1
) and (
4.1
). Inspired by the work
[
47
]
, by using Fourier splitting method, after some iterations of time decay rates, we establish the
H^{1}
-asymptotic behavior of global large strong solutions, where the
L^{2}
-decay rate is optimal. In order to establish the optimal decay rates of the first-order derivatives of the solution, we find a key observation from
[
15
]
that the solution will be sufficiently small provided that time is large enough. Therefore, our strategy is to derive energy estimates of the following type

\frac{{\mathrm{d}}}{{\mathrm{d}}t}\mathcal{E}(t)+C\left(\|\nabla^{2}u\|_{H^{1}}^{2}+\|\nabla^{2}\theta\|_{H^{1}}^{2}+\|\nabla^{2}\rho\|_{L^{2}}^{2}\right)\leq C\mathcal{N}(t)\left(\|\nabla^{2}u\|_{H^{1}}^{2}+\|\nabla^{2}\theta\|_{H^{1}}^{2}+\|\nabla^{2}\rho\|_{L^{2}}^{2}\right),

(1.24)

with
\mathcal{E}(t)
equivalent to
\|(\nabla\rho,\nabla u,\nabla\theta)\|_{H^{1}}^{2}
, where
\mathcal{N}(t)
consists of the lower-order terms, and converges to zero as time goes to infinity, see (
4.80
)–(
4.81
) for details. Based on (
1.24
), we can derive the desired decay estimate with the help of Fourier splitting method. However, the above argument cannot be applied directly to establish the optimal decay estimate of the second-order derivatives of the solution. Since that (
1.24
) is established through a suitable linear combination of first-order, second-order energy estimates, as well as a dissipative estimate of
\|\nabla^{2}\rho\|_{L^{2}}
, where the second-order energy estimate

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\frac{{\mathrm{d}}}{{\mathrm{d}}t}\left(R\bar{\rho}^{-2}\|\nabla^{2}\rho\|_{L^{2}}^{2}+\|\nabla^{2}u\|_{L^{2}}^{2}+\bar{\theta}\|\nabla^{2}\theta\|_{L^{2}}^{2}\right)+\mu\bar{\rho}^{-1}\bar{\theta}^{\alpha}\|\nabla^{3}u\|_{L^{2}}^{2}+\kappa\bar{\rho}^{-1}\bar{\theta}^{\beta-1}\|\nabla^{3}\theta\|_{L^{2}}^{2}

\displaystyle\leq~

\displaystyle C\mathcal{N}(t)\left(\|\nabla^{2}u\|_{H^{1}}^{2}+\|\nabla^{2}\rho\|_{L^{2}}^{2}+\|\nabla^{2}\theta\|_{H^{1}}^{2}\right)

cannot be closed by itself due to the fact that
\|\nabla^{2}u\|_{H^{1}}^{2}
and
\|\nabla^{2}\theta\|_{H^{1}}^{2}
cannot be absorbed by the left hand side of the above inequality. To overcome these difficulties, we decompose the solution into low-frequency and high-frequency parts, and divide the proof into two cases,
p_{0}\in[1,2)
and
p_{0}=2
. In the former case, motivated by
[
40
]
, we find that the decay rate of the low-frequency part can be obtained with the help of Duhamel’s principle. For the high-frequency part, we derive the Lyapunov-type inequality

\frac{{\mathrm{d}}}{{\mathrm{d}}t}\mathcal{E}_{1}(t)+C\mathcal{E}_{1}(t)\leq C(1+t)^{-\frac{3}{2}(\frac{2}{p_{0}}-1)-2},

to close the above second-order energy estimate, where
\mathcal{E}_{1}(t)
is equivalent to the high-frequency part of
\|(\nabla^{2}\rho,\nabla^{2}u,\nabla^{2}\theta)\|_{L^{2}}^{2}
. In the latter case, inspired by
[
39
]
, through more accurate analysis of the solution, we succeed in establishing the following key inequality

\frac{{\mathrm{d}}}{{\mathrm{d}}t}\mathcal{E}_{2}(t)+C\left(\|(\nabla^{3}\rho)_{L}\|_{L^{2}}^{2}+\|(\nabla^{2}\rho)_{H}\|_{L^{2}}^{2}+\|\nabla^{3}u\|_{L^{2}}^{2}+\|\nabla^{3}\theta\|_{L^{2}}^{2}\right)\leq 0,

(1.25)

where
\mathcal{E}_{2}(t)
is equivalent to
\|(\nabla^{2}\rho,\nabla^{2}u,\nabla^{2}\theta)\|_{L^{2}}^{2}
, and
f_{L},f_{H}
denotes the low-frequency and high-frequency part of a function
f
, respectively. The inequality (
1.25
) implies that the low-frequency part of the density possesses a better dissipative properties than the high-frequency part. With the help of (
1.25
) and Gagliardo-Nirenberg inequality, we obtain an energy inequality

\frac{{\mathrm{d}}}{{\mathrm{d}}t}\mathcal{E}_{2}(t)+C\mathcal{E}_{2}^{\frac{3}{2}}(t)\leq 0,

which leads to the desired decay estimate. As a result, we extend the works in
[
14
,
15
,
16
,
40
,
53
,
59
,
65
]
to the nonisentropic, variable coefficients case. To the best of authors’ knowledge, this is the first result of asymptotic behavior of large strong solution to the variable viscosity fluid model.

The rest of this paper is organized as follows. In §
2
, we give some notations and preliminary lemmas which will be used frequently in our following proof. In §
3
, we establish the global existence of large strong solution by using bootstrap argument. More precisely, in §
3.1.1
, we give the lower-order uniform estimates of solution, and the higher-order time-weighted uniform estimates are given in §
3.1.2
. In §
3.1.3
, we give the uniform estimates on the first-order spatial derivatives of density, by which, lower and upper bounds of density are also obtained.
Combining the estimates mentioned above, in §
3.2
, we show the higher-order uniform estimates, then we can close our the priori estimates and extend the local solution to a global one. Finally, in §
4
, we establish the convergence rate of the solution to its far-field limit when the initial data belong to
L^{p_{0}}({\mathbb{R}}^{3})
for some
p_{0}\in[1,2]
.

2
Preliminaries

For later purpose, we introduce the following notations. For any
r\in[1,\infty]
and integer
k\geq 0
, we denote

\displaystyle L^{r}=L^{r}({\mathbb{R}}^{3}),\quad W^{k,r}=W^{k,r}({\mathbb{R}}^{3}),\quad H^{k}=W^{k,2},

\displaystyle D^{k,r}=\left\{u\in L^{1}_{loc}({\mathbb{R}}^{3}):~\nabla^{k}u\in L^{r}\right\}.

For matrices
A
and
B
, we denote
A:B=\sum\limits_{i,j=1}^{n}A_{ij}B_{ij}
and
|A|^{2}=A:A
, where
A_{ij}
is the
(i,j)
-element of
A
. For any function
f(x,t)
, we denote

\int f{\mathrm{d}}x=\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{3}}f{\mathrm{d}}x,

and
\dot{f}=f_{t}+u\cdot\nabla f
is the material derivative of
f
. Moreover,
\|\cdot\|_{L^{p}}
,
\|\cdot\|_{L^{\infty}}
and
\|\cdot\|_{H^{s}}
stand for the norms of
L^{p}
,
L^{\infty}
and
H^{s}
, respectively.
\mathcal{F}(f)
or
\widehat{f}(\xi)
denote the Fourier transformation of a function
f(x)
. Throughout this paper, we use
C
to denote a generic positive constant that may depend on
\mu
,
\lambda
,
\kappa
,
R
,
C_{0}
, but is independent of
T
,
\bar{\rho}
,
\bar{\theta}
,
K_{i}
, and will change in different places.

The following local existence result can be similarly obtained to
[
43
,
64
]
.

Proposition 2.1
.

Under the condition of Theorem
1.1
, there exist
T_{*}>0
and a strong solution
(\rho,u,\theta)
to (
1.15
)–(
1.16
) in
{\mathbb{R}}^{3}\times[0,T_{*}]
satisfying (
1.19
)–(
1.20
).

The following well-known Gagliardo-Nirenberg inequality will be used (see
[
31
]
).

Lemma 2.2
.

For
p\in[2,6]
,
q\in(1,\infty)
and
r\in(3,\infty)
, there exists a constant
C>0
which may depend on
q,r
such that for
f\in H^{1}
and
g\in L^{q}\cap D^{1,r}
, it holds

\|f\|_{L^{p}}\leq C\|f\|_{L^{2}}^{\frac{6-p}{2p}}\|\nabla f\|_{L^{2}}^{\frac{3p-6}{2p}},

(2.1)

\|g\|_{L^{\infty}}\leq C\|g\|_{L^{q}}^{\frac{q(r-3)}{3r+q(r-3)}}\|\nabla g\|_{L^{r}}^{\frac{3r}{3r+q(r-3)}}.

(2.2)

From
(\ref{FCNS:2})_{2}
, we introduce the modified effective viscous flux
G
,

G=(2\mu+\lambda){\rm div}u-R(\rho\theta^{1-\alpha}-\bar{\rho}\bar{\theta}^{1-\alpha}),

(2.3)

which satisfies

-\Delta G={\rm div}H,

(2.4)

where

H=\theta^{-\alpha}\left(-\rho\dot{u}+R\alpha\rho\nabla\theta+2\mu\mathfrak{D}(u)\cdot\nabla(\theta^{\alpha})+\lambda{\rm div}u\nabla(\theta^{\alpha})\right).

(2.5)

Then we have the following elementary estimates:

Lemma 2.3
.

Let
(\rho,u,\theta)
be a smooth solution of (
1.15
)–(
1.16
). Then there exists a generic positive constant
C
depending only on
\mu
,
\lambda
,
\kappa
, and
R
such that, for any
p\in[2,6]
, it holds

\|\nabla G\|_{L^{p}}\leq C\|H\|_{L^{p}}\leq C\bar{\theta}^{-\alpha}\left(\|\rho\dot{u}\|_{L^{p}}+\bar{\rho}\|\nabla\theta\|_{L^{p}}+\bar{\theta}^{\alpha-1}\|\nabla u\cdot\nabla\theta\|_{L^{p}}\right),

(2.6)

\|G\|_{L^{p}}\leq C\|H\|_{L^{2}}^{\frac{3p-6}{2p}}\left(\|\nabla u\|_{L^{2}}+\|\rho\theta^{1-\alpha}-\bar{\rho}\bar{\theta}^{1-\alpha}\|_{L^{2}}\right)^{\frac{6-p}{2p}},

(2.7)

\|\nabla u\|_{L^{p}}\leq C\|\nabla u\|_{L^{2}}^{\frac{6-p}{2p}}\left(\|H\|_{L^{2}}+\|\rho\theta^{1-\alpha}-\bar{\rho}\bar{\theta}^{1-\alpha}\|_{L^{6}}\right)^{\frac{3p-6}{2p}}.

(2.8)

Proof.

Applying standard
L^{p}
-estimate to (
2.4
) and noting the definition of
H
in (
2.5
), we obtain (
2.6
).

By the interpolation inequalities of
L^{p}
spaces, we have

\displaystyle\|G\|_{L^{p}}\leq\|\nabla G\|_{L^{2}}^{\frac{3p-6}{2p}}\|G\|_{L^{2}}^{\frac{6-p}{2p}},

(2.9)

then (
2.7
) is followed by (
2.6
) and (
2.9
).

Noting that
-\Delta u=-\nabla{\rm div}u+\nabla\times\nabla\times u
,
we get

\displaystyle-\nabla u=-\nabla(-\Delta)^{-1}\nabla{\rm div}u+\nabla(-\Delta)^{-1}\nabla\times\nabla\times u.

Therefore, standard
L^{p}
-estimate implies

\displaystyle\|\nabla u\|_{L^{p}}\leq C\left(\|{\rm div}u\|_{L^{p}}+\|\nabla\times u\|_{L^{p}}\right).

(2.10)

By the definition of
G
in (
2.3
) and (
2.10
), we obtain

\|\nabla u\|_{L^{p}}\leq C\left(\|G\|_{L^{p}}+\|\nabla\times u\|_{L^{p}}+\|\rho\theta^{1-\alpha}-\bar{\rho}\bar{\theta}^{1-\alpha}\|_{L^{p}}\right).

(2.11)

Noting that
\mu\Delta(\nabla\times u)=\nabla\times H
, we have

\|\nabla\times u\|_{L^{p}}\leq C\|H\|_{L^{2}}^{\frac{3p-6}{2p}}\left(\|\nabla u\|_{L^{2}}+\|\rho\theta^{1-\alpha}-\bar{\rho}\bar{\theta}^{1-\alpha}\|_{L^{2}}\right)^{\frac{6-p}{2p}},

(2.12)

then (
2.8
) is followed by (
2.11
), (
2.7
), (
2.12
), and the interpolation inequalities of
L^{p}
spaces.
∎

3
Global Existence of Large Strong Solution

In this section, we will establish a priori bounds for local-in-time strong solution to (
1.15
)–(
1.16
) obtained in Proposition
2
. We thus fix a strong solution

(\rho,u,\theta)
of (
1.15
)–(
1.16
) on
{\mathbb{R}}^{3}\times(0,T]
for some time
T>0
, with initial data

(\rho_{0},u_{0},\theta_{0})
satisfying (
1.17
).
For
\sigma(t):=\min\{1,t\}
, we define
A_{i}(T)
and
B_{i}(T)
as follows:

A_{1}(T)=\bar{\theta}^{\alpha}\sup\limits_{t\in[0,T]}\|\nabla u\|_{L^{2}}^{2}+\int_{0}^{T}\|\sqrt{\rho}\dot{u}\|_{L^{2}}^{2}{\mathrm{d}}t,\quad A_{2}(T)=\bar{\theta}^{\beta}\sup\limits_{t\in[0,T]}\|\nabla\theta\|_{L^{2}}^{2}+\int_{0}^{T}\|\sqrt{\rho}\dot{\theta}\|_{L^{2}}^{2}{\mathrm{d}}t,

(3.1)

A_{3}(T)=\sup\limits_{t\in[0,T]}\|\sqrt{\rho}\dot{u}\|_{L^{2}}^{2}+\bar{\theta}^{\alpha}\int_{0}^{T}\|\nabla\dot{u}\|_{L^{2}}^{2}{\mathrm{d}}t,\quad A_{4}(T)=\sup\limits_{t\in[0,T]}\|\sqrt{\rho}\dot{\theta}\|_{L^{2}}^{2}+\bar{\theta}^{\beta}\int_{0}^{T}\|\nabla\dot{\theta}\|_{L^{2}}^{2}{\mathrm{d}}t,

(3.2)

A_{5}(T)=\sup_{t\in[0,T]}\|\nabla\rho\|_{L^{2}}^{2}+\bar{\rho}\bar{\theta}^{1-\alpha}\int_{0}^{T}\|\nabla\rho\|_{L^{2}}^{2}{\mathrm{d}}t,\quad A_{6}(T)=\sup_{t\in[0,T]}\|\nabla\rho\|_{L^{4}}^{2}+\bar{\rho}\bar{\theta}^{1-\alpha}\int_{0}^{T}\|\nabla\rho\|_{L^{4}}^{2}{\mathrm{d}}t,

(3.3)

B_{1}(T)=\bar{\theta}^{\alpha}\sup\limits_{t\in[0,T]}(\sigma^{3}\|\nabla u\|_{L^{2}}^{2})+\int_{0}^{T}\sigma^{3}\|\sqrt{\rho}\dot{u}\|_{L^{2}}^{2}{\mathrm{d}}t,~B_{2}(T)=\bar{\theta}^{\beta}\sup\limits_{t\in[0,T]}(\sigma^{4}\|\nabla\theta\|_{L^{2}}^{2})+\int_{0}^{T}\sigma^{4}\|\sqrt{\rho}\dot{\theta}\|_{L^{2}}^{2}{\mathrm{d}}t,

(3.4)

B_{3}(T)=\sup\limits_{t\in[0,T]}(\sigma^{5}\|\sqrt{\rho}\dot{u}\|_{L^{2}}^{2})+\bar{\theta}^{\alpha}\int_{0}^{T}\sigma^{5}\|\nabla\dot{u}\|_{L^{2}}^{2}{\mathrm{d}}t,~B_{4}(T)=\sup\limits_{t\in[0,T]}(\sigma^{6}\|\sqrt{\rho}\dot{\theta}\|_{L^{2}}^{2})+\bar{\theta}^{\beta}\int_{0}^{T}\sigma^{6}\|\nabla\dot{\theta}\|_{L^{2}}^{2}{\mathrm{d}}t.

(3.5)

We then have the following key a priori estimates on
(\rho,u,\theta)
.

Proposition 3.1
.

For given
\bar{\rho},\bar{\theta}>1
, assume that
(\rho_{0},u_{0},\theta_{0})
satisfies (
1.17
)
and (
1.18
), then there exist positive constants
K_{i}
,
\widetilde{L}
and
L
depending on
\mu
,
\lambda
,
\kappa
,
R
,
C_{0}
, such that if
(\rho,u,\theta)
is a strong solution of (
1.15
)–(
1.16
) on
{\mathbb{R}}^{3}\times(0,T]
satisfying

A_{1}(\sigma(T))\leq 2K_{1}\bar{\theta}^{\alpha},~A_{2}(\sigma(T))\leq 2K_{2}\bar{\theta}^{\beta},~A_{3}(\sigma(T))\leq 2K_{3}\bar{\theta}^{2\alpha},~A_{4}(\sigma(T))\leq 2K_{4}\bar{\theta}^{2\beta},

(3.6)

\displaystyle B_{1}(T)\leq 2K_{5}\bar{\rho}\bar{\theta},\quad B_{2}(T)\leq 2K_{6}\bar{\rho}\bar{\theta}^{2},\quad B_{3}(T)\leq 2\bar{\theta}^{\frac{\alpha}{3}+\frac{2}{3}},

(3.7)

\displaystyle B_{4}(T)\leq 2K_{7}\bar{\rho}\bar{\theta}^{2},\quad A_{5}(T)\leq 2K_{8},\quad A_{6}(T)\leq 2K_{9},

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\bar{\theta}\leq\theta\leq\frac{3}{2}\bar{\theta},\quad\frac{1}{2}\bar{\rho}\leq\rho\leq\frac{3}{2}\bar{\rho},\qquad\text{for}~t\in[0,T],~x\in{\mathbb{R}}^{3},

then the following estimates hold

A_{1}(\sigma(T))\leq K_{1}\bar{\theta}^{\alpha},~A_{2}(\sigma(T))\leq K_{2}\bar{\theta}^{\beta},~A_{3}(\sigma(T))\leq K_{3}\bar{\theta}^{2\alpha},~A_{4}(\sigma(T))\leq K_{4}\bar{\theta}^{2\beta},

(3.8)

\displaystyle B_{1}(T)\leq K_{5}\bar{\rho}\bar{\theta},\quad B_{2}(T)\leq K_{6}\bar{\rho}\bar{\theta}^{2},\quad B_{3}(T)\leq\bar{\theta}^{\frac{\alpha}{3}+\frac{2}{3}},

(3.9)

\displaystyle B_{4}(T)\leq K_{7}\bar{\rho}\bar{\theta}^{2},\quad A_{5}(T)\leq K_{8},\quad A_{6}(T)\leq K_{9},

\displaystyle\frac{2}{3}\bar{\theta}\leq\theta\leq\frac{4}{3}\bar{\theta},\quad\frac{2}{3}\bar{\rho}\leq\rho\leq\frac{4}{3}\bar{\rho},\qquad\text{for}~t\in[0,T],~x\in{\mathbb{R}}^{3},

provided that
\bar{\rho}\geq\widetilde{L}
,
\bar{\theta}\geq L
and

C(K)\bar{\rho}\leq\min\left\{\bar{\theta}^{2(\beta-\alpha)},\bar{\theta}^{2(\alpha-\beta+2)},\bar{\theta}^{\frac{1}{3}}\right\},

(3.10)

with some positive constant
C(K)
depending on
K_{i}~(i=1,\cdots,9)
.

Next, we give some lemmas to prove Proposition
3
.

3.1
Proof of Proposition
3

In this subsection, we use
C(K)
to denote a constant that may depend on
K_{i}~(i=1,\cdots,9)
. We begin with the following standard energy estimate of
(\rho,u,\theta)
.

Lemma 3.2
.

Under the conditions of Proposition
3
, there exists a positive constant
C
depending on
\mu
,
\lambda
,
\kappa
,
C_{0}
, and
R
such that if
(\rho,u,\theta)
is a smooth solution of (
1.15
)–(
1.16
) on
{\mathbb{R}}^{3}\times(0,T]
, the following estimates hold for all
t\in(0,T]:

\|\rho-\bar{\rho}\|_{L^{2}}^{2}\leq C\bar{\rho}^{2},\qquad\|u\|_{L^{2}}^{2}\leq C\bar{\theta},\qquad\|\theta-\bar{\theta}\|_{L^{2}}^{2}\leq C\bar{\theta}^{2},

(3.11)

\int_{0}^{T}\|\nabla u\|_{L^{2}}^{2}{\mathrm{d}}t\leq C\bar{\rho}\bar{\theta}^{1-\alpha},\qquad\int_{0}^{T}\|\nabla\theta\|_{L^{2}}^{2}{\mathrm{d}}t\leq C\bar{\rho}\bar{\theta}^{2-\beta}.

(3.12)

Proof.

Adding
(\ref{FCNS:2})_{2}
multiplied by
u
to
(\ref{FCNS:2})_{3}
multiplied by
\displaystyle{1-\frac{\bar{\theta}}{\theta}}
, we obtain after integrating the resulting equality over
{\mathbb{R}}^{3}
and using
(\ref{FCNS:2})_{1}
that

\displaystyle\frac{{\mathrm{d}}}{{\mathrm{d}}t}\int\left(\frac{1}{2}\rho|u|^{2}+R\bar{\theta}(\rho\ln\rho-\rho-\rho\ln\bar{\rho}+\bar{\rho})+\rho(\theta-\bar{\theta}\ln\theta-\bar{\theta}+\bar{\theta}\ln\bar{\theta})\right){\mathrm{d}}x

\displaystyle=~

\displaystyle-\int\left(\lambda\theta^{\alpha}({\rm div}u)^{2}+2\mu\theta^{\alpha}|\mathfrak{D}(u)|^{2}\right){\mathrm{d}}x-\int\frac{\kappa\bar{\theta}}{\theta^{2-\beta}}|\nabla\theta|^{2}{\mathrm{d}}x+\int\left(1-\frac{\bar{\theta}}{\theta}\right)\left(\lambda\theta^{\alpha}({\rm div}u)^{2}+2\mu\theta^{\alpha}|\mathfrak{D}(u)|^{2}\right){\mathrm{d}}x

\displaystyle=~

\displaystyle-\int\left(\bar{\theta}\left(\lambda\theta^{\alpha-1}({\rm div}u)^{2}+2\mu\theta^{\alpha-1}|\mathfrak{D}(u)|^{2}\right)+\frac{\kappa\bar{\theta}}{\theta^{2-\beta}}|\nabla\theta|^{2}\right){\mathrm{d}}x.

(3.13)

It follows from the identity

\rho\ln\rho-\rho-\rho\ln\bar{\rho}+\bar{\rho}=(\rho-\bar{\rho})^{2}\int_{0}^{1}\frac{1-y}{y(\rho-\bar{\rho})+\bar{\rho}}{\mathrm{d}}y

that

(10\bar{\rho})^{-1}(\rho-\bar{\rho})^{2}\leq\rho\ln\rho-\rho-\rho\ln\bar{\rho}+\bar{\rho}\leq 2\bar{\rho}^{-1}(\rho-\bar{\rho})^{2}.

(3.14)

Similarly, it follows from the identity

\theta-\bar{\theta}\ln\theta-\bar{\theta}+\bar{\theta}\ln\bar{\theta}=(\theta-\bar{\theta})^{2}\int_{0}^{1}\frac{y}{y(\theta-\bar{\theta})+\bar{\theta}}{\mathrm{d}}y

that

(10\bar{\theta})^{-1}(\theta-\bar{\theta})^{2}\leq\theta-\bar{\theta}\ln\theta-\bar{\theta}+\bar{\theta}\ln\bar{\theta}\leq 2\bar{\theta}^{-1}(\theta-\bar{\theta})^{2}.

(3.15)

Integrating (
3.1
) with respect to
t
over
(0,T)
and using (
1.18
) yields

\displaystyle\sup\limits_{t\in[0,T]}\int\left(\frac{1}{2}\rho|u|^{2}+R\bar{\theta}(\rho\ln\rho-\rho-\rho\ln\bar{\rho}+\bar{\rho})+\rho(\theta-\bar{\theta}\ln\theta-\bar{\theta}+\bar{\theta}\ln\bar{\theta})\right){\mathrm{d}}x

\displaystyle+\int_{0}^{T}\int\left(\bar{\theta}\left(\lambda\theta^{\alpha-1}({\rm div}u)^{2}+2\mu\theta^{\alpha-1}|\mathfrak{D}(u)|^{2}\right)+\frac{\kappa\bar{\theta}}{\theta^{2-\beta}}|\nabla\theta|^{2}\right){\mathrm{d}}x{\mathrm{d}}t\leq C\bar{\rho}\bar{\theta},

(3.16)

which together with (
3.14
) and (
3.15
) leads to

\displaystyle\sup\limits_{t\in[0,T]}\int\left(\rho|u|^{2}+\bar{\rho}^{-1}\bar{\theta}(\rho-\bar{\rho})^{2}+\rho\bar{\theta}^{-1}(\theta-\bar{\theta})^{2}\right){\mathrm{d}}x

\displaystyle+\int_{0}^{T}\int\left(\bar{\theta}\left(\lambda\theta^{\alpha-1}({\rm div}u)^{2}+2\mu\theta^{\alpha-1}|\mathfrak{D}(u)|^{2}\right)+\frac{\kappa\bar{\theta}}{\theta^{2-\beta}}|\nabla\theta|^{2}\right){\mathrm{d}}x{\mathrm{d}}t\leq C\bar{\rho}\bar{\theta}.

(3.17)

This directly gives (
3.11
) and (
3.12
).
∎

3.1.1
Uniform estimates of velocity and temperature on
[0,\sigma(T)]

The following lemma will give estimates on
A_{1}(\sigma(T))
and
A_{2}(\sigma(T))
.

Lemma 3.3
.

Under the conditions of Proposition
3
, there exists positive constants
K_{1}
,
K_{2}
and
L_{1}
depending on
\mu
,
\lambda
,
\kappa
,
R
, and
C_{0}
, such that if
(\rho,u,\theta)
is a smooth solution of (
1.15
)–(
1.16
) on
{\mathbb{R}}^{3}\times(0,T]
, the following estimate holds

A_{1}(\sigma(T))=\bar{\theta}^{\alpha}\sup\limits_{t\in[0,\sigma(T)]}\|\nabla u\|_{L^{2}}^{2}+\int_{0}^{\sigma(T)}\|\sqrt{\rho}\dot{u}\|_{L^{2}}^{2}{\mathrm{d}}t\leq K_{1}\bar{\theta}^{\alpha},

(3.18)

and

A_{2}(\sigma(T))=\bar{\theta}^{\beta}\sup\limits_{t\in[0,\sigma(T)]}\|\nabla\theta\|_{L^{2}}^{2}+\int_{0}^{\sigma(T)}\|\sqrt{\rho}\dot{\theta}\|_{L^{2}}^{2}{\mathrm{d}}t\leq K_{2}\bar{\theta}^{\beta},

(3.19)

provided that
\bar{\theta}\geq L_{1}
,
2\leq\alpha<\beta<\alpha+2
, and

C(K)\bar{\rho}\leq\min\left\{\bar{\theta}^{\alpha-1},\bar{\theta}^{\frac{1}{2}(3\alpha-\beta-1)},\bar{\theta}^{2(\alpha-\beta+2)},\bar{\theta}^{\frac{1}{2}(\alpha+\beta-3)},\bar{\theta}^{2(\beta-\alpha)}\right\}.

(3.20)

Proof.

First, multiplying
(\ref{FCNS:2})_{2}
by
2u_{t}
and integrating the resulting equality over
{\mathbb{R}}^{3}
, we obtain after integration by parts and using (
2.3
) that

\displaystyle\frac{{\mathrm{d}}}{{\mathrm{d}}t}\int\left(2\mu\theta^{\alpha}|\mathfrak{D}(u)|^{2}+\lambda\theta^{\alpha}({\rm div}u)^{2}\right){\mathrm{d}}x+\int\rho|u_{t}|^{2}{\mathrm{d}}x

\displaystyle\leq~

\displaystyle-2\int\nabla P\cdot u_{t}{\mathrm{d}}x+\int\rho|u\cdot\nabla u|^{2}{\mathrm{d}}x-\alpha\int\theta^{\alpha-1}\theta_{t}\left(2\mu|\mathfrak{D}(u)|^{2}+\lambda({\rm div}u)^{2}\right){\mathrm{d}}x

\displaystyle=~

\displaystyle 2R\frac{{\mathrm{d}}}{{\mathrm{d}}t}\int(\rho\theta-\bar{\rho}\bar{\theta}){\rm div}u{\mathrm{d}}x-2\int P_{t}{\rm div}u{\mathrm{d}}x+\int\rho|u\cdot\nabla u|^{2}{\mathrm{d}}x-\alpha\int\theta^{\alpha-1}\theta_{t}\left(2\mu|\mathfrak{D}(u)|^{2}+\lambda({\rm div}u)^{2}\right){\mathrm{d}}x

\displaystyle=~

\displaystyle 2R\frac{{\mathrm{d}}}{{\mathrm{d}}t}\int(\rho\theta-\bar{\rho}\bar{\theta}){\rm div}u{\mathrm{d}}x-\frac{2}{2\mu+\lambda}\int P_{t}G{\mathrm{d}}x+\int\rho|u\cdot\nabla u|^{2}{\mathrm{d}}x

\displaystyle-\alpha\int\theta^{\alpha-1}\theta_{t}\left(2\mu|\mathfrak{D}(u)|^{2}+\lambda({\rm div}u)^{2}\right){\mathrm{d}}x-\frac{2R}{2\mu+\lambda}\int P_{t}(\rho\theta^{1-\alpha}-\bar{\rho}\bar{\theta}^{1-\alpha}){\mathrm{d}}x

\displaystyle:=~

\displaystyle 2R\frac{{\mathrm{d}}}{{\mathrm{d}}t}\int(\rho\theta-\bar{\rho}\bar{\theta}){\rm div}u{\mathrm{d}}x+\sum\limits_{i=1}^{4}I_{i}.

(3.21)

Next we estimate the terms on the right hand side of (
3.1.1
). Noticing that (
1.15
) implies

P_{t}=-{\rm div}(Pu)+R\rho\dot{\theta},

(3.22)

after integration by parts and using (
2.7
), (
3.6
), and Lemma
3.1
, we obtain

\displaystyle I_{1}=~

\displaystyle-\frac{2}{2\mu+\lambda}\int P_{t}G{\mathrm{d}}x

\displaystyle=~

\displaystyle-\frac{2}{2\mu+\lambda}\int\left(-{\rm div}(Pu)+R\rho\dot{\theta}\right)G{\mathrm{d}}x

\displaystyle\leq~

\displaystyle C\int P|u||\nabla G|{\mathrm{d}}x+C\bar{\rho}^{\frac{1}{2}}\int|\sqrt{\rho}\dot{\theta}||G|{\mathrm{d}}x

\displaystyle\leq~

\displaystyle C\left(\bar{\rho}^{\frac{1}{2}}\bar{\theta}\|\sqrt{\rho}u\|_{L^{2}}\|\nabla G\|_{L^{2}}+\bar{\rho}^{\frac{1}{2}}\|\sqrt{\rho}\dot{\theta}\|_{L^{2}}\|G\|_{L^{2}}\right)

\displaystyle\leq~

\displaystyle C\left(\bar{\rho}\bar{\theta}\|u\|_{L^{2}}\|\nabla G\|_{L^{2}}+\bar{\rho}^{\frac{1}{2}}\|\sqrt{\rho}\dot{\theta}\|_{L^{2}}\|\nabla u\|_{L^{2}}+\bar{\rho}^{\frac{1}{2}}\bar{\theta}^{1-\alpha}\|\sqrt{\rho}\dot{\theta}\|_{L^{2}}\|\rho-\bar{\rho}\|_{L^{2}}+\bar{\rho}^{\frac{3}{2}}\bar{\theta}^{-\alpha}\|\sqrt{\rho}\dot{\theta}\|_{L^{2}}\|\theta-\bar{\theta}\|_{L^{2}}\right)

\displaystyle\leq~

\displaystyle C\left(\bar{\rho}\bar{\theta}^{\frac{3}{2}}\|\nabla G\|_{L^{2}}+\bar{\rho}^{\frac{1}{2}}\|\sqrt{\rho}\dot{\theta}\|_{L^{2}}\|\nabla u\|_{L^{2}}+\bar{\rho}^{\frac{3}{2}}\bar{\theta}^{1-\alpha}\|\sqrt{\rho}\dot{\theta}\|_{L^{2}}\right).

(3.23)

where we have used the estimates

\displaystyle\|G\|_{L^{2}}\leq~

\displaystyle C\left(\|\nabla u\|_{L^{2}}+\|\rho\theta^{1-\alpha}-\bar{\rho}\bar{\theta}^{1-\alpha}\|_{L^{2}}\right)

\displaystyle\leq~

\displaystyle C\left(\|\nabla u\|_{L^{2}}+\bar{\theta}^{-\alpha}\|\rho(\theta-\bar{\theta})\|_{L^{2}}+\bar{\theta}^{1-\alpha}\|\rho-\bar{\rho}\|_{L^{2}}\right)

\displaystyle\leq~

\displaystyle C\left(\|\nabla u\|_{L^{2}}+\bar{\rho}\bar{\theta}^{1-\alpha}\right),

(3.24)

\displaystyle I_{2}=\int\rho|u\cdot\nabla u|^{2}{\mathrm{d}}x\leq C\bar{\rho}\|u\|_{L^{6}}^{2}\|\nabla u\|_{L^{2}}\|\nabla u\|_{L^{6}}\leq C\bar{\rho}\|\nabla u\|_{L^{2}}^{3}\|\nabla u\|_{L^{6}},

(3.25)

\displaystyle I_{3}=~

\displaystyle-\alpha\int\theta^{\alpha-1}\theta_{t}\left(2\mu|\mathfrak{D}(u)|^{2}+\lambda({\rm div}u)^{2}\right){\mathrm{d}}x

\displaystyle\leq~

\displaystyle C\bar{\theta}^{\alpha-1}\int|\theta_{t}||\nabla u|^{2}{\mathrm{d}}x

\displaystyle\leq~

\displaystyle C\bar{\rho}^{-\frac{1}{2}}\bar{\theta}^{\alpha-1}\|\sqrt{\rho}\dot{\theta}\|_{L^{2}}\|\nabla u\|_{L^{2}}^{\frac{1}{2}}\|\nabla u\|_{L^{6}}^{\frac{3}{2}}+C\bar{\rho}^{-\frac{1}{2}}\bar{\theta}^{\alpha-1}\|u\|_{L^{\infty}}\|\nabla\theta\|_{L^{2}}\|\nabla u\|_{L^{2}}^{\frac{1}{2}}\|\nabla u\|_{L^{6}}^{\frac{3}{2}}

\displaystyle\leq~

\displaystyle C\bar{\rho}^{-\frac{1}{2}}\bar{\theta}^{\alpha-1}\|\sqrt{\rho}\dot{\theta}\|_{L^{2}}\|\nabla u\|_{L^{2}}^{\frac{1}{2}}\|\nabla u\|_{L^{6}}^{\frac{3}{2}}+C\bar{\rho}^{-\frac{1}{2}}\bar{\theta}^{\alpha-1}\|\nabla\theta\|_{L^{2}}\|\nabla u\|_{L^{2}}\|\nabla u\|_{L^{6}}^{2}.

(3.26)

After integration by parts and using (
3.22
), (
3.7
) and Lemma

, we obtain

\displaystyle I_{4}=~

\displaystyle-\frac{2R}{2\mu+\lambda}\int P_{t}(\rho\theta^{1-\alpha}-\bar{\rho}\bar{\theta}^{1-\alpha}){\mathrm{d}}x

\displaystyle=~

\displaystyle-\frac{2R}{2\mu+\lambda}\int\left(-{\rm div}(Pu)+R\rho\dot{\theta}\right)(\rho\theta^{1-\alpha}-\bar{\rho}\bar{\theta}^{1-\alpha}){\mathrm{d}}x

\displaystyle\leq~

\displaystyle C\bar{\rho}^{\frac{1}{2}}\bar{\theta}^{2-\alpha}\int|\sqrt{\rho}u||\nabla\rho|{\mathrm{d}}x+C\bar{\rho}^{\frac{3}{2}}\bar{\theta}^{1-\alpha}\int|\sqrt{\rho}u||\nabla\theta|{\mathrm{d}}x+C\bar{\rho}^{\frac{1}{2}}\int|\sqrt{\rho}\dot{\theta}|\left|\rho\theta^{1-\alpha}-\bar{\rho}\bar{\theta}^{1-\alpha}\right|{\mathrm{d}}x

\displaystyle\leq~

\displaystyle C\bar{\rho}^{\frac{1}{2}}\bar{\theta}^{2-\alpha}\|\sqrt{\rho}u\|_{L^{2}}\|\nabla\rho\|_{L^{2}}+C\bar{\rho}^{\frac{3}{2}}\bar{\theta}^{1-\alpha}\|\sqrt{\rho}u\|_{L^{2}}\|\nabla\theta\|_{L^{2}}+C\bar{\rho}^{\frac{1}{2}}\|\sqrt{\rho}\dot{\theta}\|_{L^{2}}\left\|\rho\theta^{1-\alpha}-\bar{\rho}\bar{\theta}^{1-\alpha}\right\|_{L^{2}}

\displaystyle\leq~

\displaystyle C\bar{\rho}\bar{\theta}^{\frac{5}{2}-\alpha}\|\nabla\rho\|_{L^{2}}+C\bar{\rho}^{\frac{1}{2}}\bar{\theta}^{\frac{3}{2}-\alpha}\|\nabla\theta\|_{L^{2}}+C\bar{\rho}^{\frac{3}{2}}\bar{\theta}^{1-\alpha}\|\sqrt{\rho}\dot{\theta}\|_{L^{2}}.

(3.27)

Substituting (
3.1.1
)–(
3.1.1
) into (
3.1.1
), integrating the resulting inequalities over

and using (

\displaystyle\bar{\theta}^{\alpha}\sup\limits_{t\in[0,\sigma(T)]}\|\nabla u\|_{L^{2}}^{2}+\int_{0}^{\sigma(T)}\|\sqrt{\rho}\dot{u}\|_{L^{2}}^{2}{\mathrm{d}}t

\displaystyle\leq~

\displaystyle C\bar{\theta}^{\alpha}\|\nabla u_{0}\|_{L^{2}}^{2}+C\|\rho\theta-\bar{\rho}\bar{\theta}\|_{L^{2}}\|\nabla u\|_{L^{2}}+C\|\rho_{0}\theta_{0}-\bar{\rho}\bar{\theta}\|_{L^{2}}\|\nabla u_{0}\|_{L^{2}}

\displaystyle+C\int_{0}^{\sigma(T)}\left(\bar{\rho}\bar{\theta}^{\frac{3}{2}}\|\nabla G\|_{L^{2}}+\bar{\rho}^{\frac{1}{2}}\|\sqrt{\rho}\dot{\theta}\|_{L^{2}}\|\nabla u\|_{L^{2}}+\bar{\rho}^{\frac{3}{2}}\bar{\theta}^{1-\alpha}\|\sqrt{\rho}\dot{\theta}\|_{L^{2}}\right){\mathrm{d}}t+C\bar{\rho}\int_{0}^{\sigma(T)}\|\nabla u\|_{L^{2}}^{3}\|\nabla u\|_{L^{6}}{\mathrm{d}}t

\displaystyle+C\bar{\rho}^{-\frac{1}{2}}\bar{\theta}^{\alpha-1}\int_{0}^{\sigma(T)}\left(\|\sqrt{\rho}\dot{\theta}\|_{L^{2}}\|\nabla u\|_{L^{2}}^{\frac{1}{2}}\|\nabla u\|_{L^{6}}^{\frac{3}{2}}+\|\nabla\theta\|_{L^{2}}\|\nabla u\|_{L^{2}}\|\nabla u\|_{L^{6}}^{2}\right){\mathrm{d}}t

\displaystyle+C\int_{0}^{\sigma(T)}\left(\bar{\rho}\bar{\theta}^{\frac{5}{2}-\alpha}\|\nabla\rho\|_{L^{2}}+\bar{\rho}^{\frac{1}{2}}\bar{\theta}^{\frac{3}{2}-\alpha}\|\nabla\theta\|_{L^{2}}\right){\mathrm{d}}t

\displaystyle\leq~

\displaystyle C\bar{\theta}^{\alpha}\|\nabla u_{0}\|_{L^{2}}^{2}+CK_{1}^{\frac{1}{2}}\bar{\rho}\bar{\theta}+CK_{2}^{\frac{1}{2}}\left(\bar{\rho}\bar{\theta}^{\frac{\beta-\alpha+1}{2}}+\bar{\rho}^{\frac{3}{2}}\bar{\theta}^{\frac{\beta}{2}-\alpha+1}\right)+CK_{8}^{\frac{1}{2}}\bar{\rho}\bar{\theta}^{\frac{5}{2}-\alpha}+C\bar{\rho}\bar{\theta}^{\frac{5}{2}-\alpha-\frac{\beta}{2}}

\displaystyle+C\bar{\rho}\bar{\theta}^{\frac{3}{2}}\int_{0}^{\sigma(T)}\|\nabla G\|_{L^{2}}{\mathrm{d}}t+CK_{1}\bar{\rho}\int_{0}^{\sigma(T)}\|\nabla u\|_{L^{2}}\|\nabla u\|_{L^{6}}{\mathrm{d}}t

\displaystyle+C\bar{\rho}^{-\frac{1}{2}}\bar{\theta}^{\alpha-1}\int_{0}^{\sigma(T)}\left(K_{1}^{\frac{1}{4}}\|\sqrt{\rho}\dot{\theta}\|_{L^{2}}\|\nabla u\|_{L^{6}}^{\frac{3}{2}}+K_{1}^{\frac{1}{2}}K_{2}^{\frac{1}{2}}\|\nabla u\|_{L^{6}}^{2}\right){\mathrm{d}}t

\displaystyle:=~

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}K_{1}\bar{\theta}^{\alpha}+\sum\limits_{i=1}^{3}I_{1i},

(3.28)

provided that

where we have used

Now it remains to estimate the terms on the right-hand side of (
3.1.1
).
\|\nabla u\|_{L^{6}}
plays an important role in our analysis. It follows from (

), (

) that

\displaystyle\|\nabla u\|_{L^{6}}\leq~

\displaystyle C\left(\|H\|_{L^{2}}+\|\rho\theta^{1-\alpha}-\bar{\rho}\bar{\theta}^{1-\alpha}\|_{L^{6}}\right)

\displaystyle\leq~

\displaystyle C\left(\bar{\theta}^{-\alpha}\|\rho\dot{u}\|_{L^{2}}+\bar{\rho}\bar{\theta}^{-\alpha}\|\nabla\theta\|_{L^{2}}+\|\rho\theta^{1-\alpha}-\bar{\rho}\bar{\theta}^{1-\alpha}\|_{L^{6}}+\bar{\theta}^{-1}\|\nabla\theta\|_{L^{4}}\|\nabla u\|_{L^{4}}\right)

\displaystyle\leq~

\displaystyle C\left(\bar{\theta}^{-\alpha}\|\rho\dot{u}\|_{L^{2}}+\bar{\rho}\bar{\theta}^{-\alpha}\|\nabla\theta\|_{L^{2}}+\bar{\theta}^{-\alpha}\|\rho(\theta-\bar{\theta})\|_{L^{6}}+\bar{\theta}^{1-\alpha}\|\rho-\bar{\rho}\|_{L^{6}}\right)

\displaystyle+C\bar{\theta}^{-1}\|\nabla\theta\|_{L^{4}}\|\nabla u\|_{L^{2}}^{\frac{1}{4}}\|\nabla u\|_{L^{6}}^{\frac{3}{4}}

\displaystyle\leq~

\displaystyle C\left(\bar{\theta}^{-\alpha}\|\rho\dot{u}\|_{L^{2}}+\bar{\rho}\bar{\theta}^{-\alpha}\|\nabla\theta\|_{L^{2}}+\bar{\theta}^{1-\alpha}\|\nabla\rho\|_{L^{2}}+\bar{\theta}^{-4}\|\nabla\theta\|_{L^{4}}^{4}\|\nabla u\|_{L^{2}}\right)+\frac{1}{2}\|\nabla u\|_{L^{6}}

\displaystyle\leq~

\displaystyle C\left(\bar{\rho}^{\frac{1}{2}}\bar{\theta}^{-\alpha}\|\sqrt{\rho}\dot{u}\|_{L^{2}}+\bar{\rho}\bar{\theta}^{-\alpha}\|\nabla\theta\|_{L^{2}}+\bar{\theta}^{1-\alpha}\|\nabla\rho\|_{L^{2}}+\bar{\theta}^{-4}\|\nabla\theta\|_{L^{2}}\|\nabla^{2}\theta\|_{L^{2}}^{3}\|\nabla u\|_{L^{2}}\right)+\frac{1}{2}\|\nabla u\|_{L^{6}}.

(3.29)

Moreover, we claim that

\displaystyle\sup\limits_{t\in[0,\sigma(T)]}\|\nabla^{2}\theta\|_{L^{2}}\leq 2\bar{\theta}.

(3.30)

Hence, from (
3.1.1
) and (

), we have

\displaystyle\|\nabla u\|_{L^{6}}

\displaystyle\leq C\left(\bar{\rho}^{\frac{1}{2}}\bar{\theta}^{-\alpha}\|\sqrt{\rho}\dot{u}\|_{L^{2}}+\bar{\rho}\bar{\theta}^{-\alpha}\|\nabla\theta\|_{L^{2}}+\bar{\theta}^{1-\alpha}\|\nabla\rho\|_{L^{2}}+\bar{\theta}^{-1}\|\nabla\theta\|_{L^{2}}\|\nabla u\|_{L^{2}}\right)

\displaystyle\leq C\left(K_{3}^{\frac{1}{2}}\bar{\rho}^{\frac{1}{2}}+K_{2}^{\frac{1}{2}}\bar{\rho}\bar{\theta}^{-\alpha}+K_{8}^{\frac{1}{2}}\bar{\theta}^{1-\alpha}+K_{1}^{\frac{1}{2}}K_{2}^{\frac{1}{2}}\bar{\theta}^{-1}\right)

\displaystyle\leq CK_{3}^{\frac{1}{2}}\bar{\rho}^{\frac{1}{2}},

(3.31)

for
t\in[0,\sigma(T)]
provided that
\alpha>1
,

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{\sigma(T)}\|\nabla u\|_{L^{6}}^{2}{\mathrm{d}}t\leq~

\displaystyle\leq~

\displaystyle C\left(K_{1}\bar{\rho}\bar{\theta}^{-\alpha}+\bar{\rho}^{3}\bar{\theta}^{-2\alpha+2-\beta}+K_{8}\bar{\theta}^{2-2\alpha}+K_{2}\bar{\rho}\bar{\theta}^{-1-\alpha}\right)

provided that

,

\displaystyle\leq~

\displaystyle\bar{\theta}^{2},

(3.33)

provided that

and

. Then (
3.30
) is proved.

Then
I_{1i}
in (
3.1.1
) can be estimated as follows:

\displaystyle I_{11}=~

\displaystyle C\bar{\rho}\bar{\theta}^{\frac{3}{2}}\int_{0}^{\sigma(T)}\|\nabla G\|_{L^{2}}{\mathrm{d}}t

\displaystyle\leq~

\displaystyle C\bar{\rho}\bar{\theta}^{\frac{3}{2}}\int_{0}^{\sigma(T)}\left(\bar{\rho}^{\frac{1}{2}}\bar{\theta}^{-\alpha}\|\sqrt{\rho}\dot{u}\|_{L^{2}}+\bar{\rho}\bar{\theta}^{-\alpha}\|\nabla\theta\|_{L^{2}}+\bar{\theta}^{-1}\|\nabla\theta\|_{L^{4}}\|\nabla u\|_{L^{4}}\right){\mathrm{d}}t

\displaystyle\leq~

\displaystyle C\bar{\rho}\bar{\theta}^{\frac{3}{2}}\left(\bar{\rho}^{\frac{1}{2}}K_{1}^{\frac{1}{2}}\bar{\theta}^{-\frac{1}{2}\alpha}+\bar{\rho}^{\frac{3}{2}}\bar{\theta}^{-\alpha+1-\frac{1}{2}\beta}+\bar{\theta}^{-1}\int_{0}^{\sigma(T)}\|\nabla\theta\|_{L^{2}}^{\frac{1}{4}}\|\nabla^{2}\theta\|_{L^{2}}^{\frac{3}{4}}\|\nabla u\|_{L^{2}}^{\frac{1}{4}}\|\nabla u\|_{L^{6}}^{\frac{3}{4}}{\mathrm{d}}t\right)

\displaystyle\leq~
